PREFACE 

Is it too much to expect that the elementary school course in arithmetic shall establish in children the power and the habit of adding, subtracting, multiplying, and dividing whole numbers, decimals, and common fractions correctly, reading and solving verbal problems accurately, and thinking quantitatively in the ordinary activities of life? Such aims are indeed beyond current realization in many schools, notwithstanding the large amount of time and effort devoted to the subject. However, in preparing the NEW DAY 

ARITHMETICS, these have been our aims-mastery in fact, rather than incomplete learning and inaccuracy and uncertainty. Lower aims in arithmetic cannot be justified.

The content and the teaching of arithmetic in the elementary school have been subjected to many scientific inquiries in the last few years. The outcomes have contributed much to our knowledge of what to teach, when to teach it, and how to teach it. As authors, we have drawn freely upon this information in so far as it has been checked by classroom use. In the case of the questions remaining for investigation, we have used the procedures which classroom experience indicates are the most effective. 

Your guide in examining this book will naturally be your background of experience in teaching or supervision. We call your attention to the provisions we have made for better teaching and better learning. iii

iv 

Preface 

1. The text is addressed to the pupil in language he can read and understand. He is, therefore, not distracted from arithmetic by difficulties in reading. The vocabulary is one with which he is already familiar. 

2. The teaching and practice material is prepared in terms of both the pupil and the subject. While each topic is introduced and developed in ways which utilize the pupil's present experience and interests, it is also developed systematically toward the more mature arithmetical understandings and skills which will function in his expanding life. 

3. The full explanations and directions provided help the pupil by indicating good ways of learning, and help the teacher by suggesting effective techniques of instruction. 

4. The treatment of each group of topics aims at (1) facility and accuracy in manipulation of numbers, and (2) understanding of number relations, the nature of the process being studied, the kinds of problem situations in which the process is used, and the steps in the process. 

Correct and economical habits of thinking and doing are cultivated from the beginning. 

5. The four processes with whole numbers, decimals, and common fractions are fully developed through their steps of understanding and skill. There is ample provision for a background of meaning in a process, as well as skill in the mechanics. Each step in a process is presented fully, explained simply, practiced intensively with examples or problems, practiced cumulatively from time to time for assimilation, and practiced in timed mixed drills for quick recall.

Preface 

6. The practice material is so organized and distributed as to result in mastery and retention, rather than incomplete learning and forgetting. Gaps in learning are disclosed by the analytical diagnostic tests and bridged by the keyed remedial practice. The practice material in an exercise is so graded as to provide for every pupil the incentive of success and to avoid the discouragement of failure. 

7. Ability to sense and solve problems is gained through consistent practice in associating the common types of number situations with the corresponding processes. The teaching of problems involving subtraction, for instance, along with the teaching of the process of subtraction—thus establishing the association of the type of situation and process-is in direct contrast to the usual artificial and ineffective teaching of problem solving. A wealth of live, interesting, arithmetically effective problem material is provided from the beginning for training in the techniques of reading problems and for practice in fitting the problems into the pupil's experience. 

8. Economy has been foremost in our thinking in making the books. But economy involves more than the pruning of obsolete topics and seldom-used numbers. The greatest waste in arithmetic lies in half learning and in forgetting. 

In so cumulative a subject, a gap is serious. Mastery of a topic before proceeding to a new topic, then, is essential. 

Frequent diagnostic testing, followed by remedial instruction and practice, is inherent in teaching for mastery. 

Hardly less important for economical learning is the accumulation of a set of efficient habits of thinking and doing. 

Provisions in the book for economy include, then, limiting

vi 

Preface the course to the intellectually and socially desirable topics, establishing a firm foundation of basic understandings, cultivating efficient habits of thinking and doing from the first, and using every means to insure mastery of a unit before proceeding to the next unit. 

9. An adequate textbook in arithmetic for use above the third grade must make provision for the wide range of abilities in individuals. In the third grade, all the understandings and skills are so basic that differentiation of learning must be in rate alone. But beyond the third grade there must be an enriched curriculum for those who can profit from it. Our books provide such enrichment. In general, a practice exercise is not only graded from easy to more difficult; it is so organized that every pupil will acquire the essentials of understanding and skill by doing the earlier examples and problems, and the quicker pupils may progress to higher levels by doing the later examples and problems. Three levels are indicated by an unobtrusive device, but the plan provides for each pupil's going as far beyond the essentials as his ability and industry will carry him. 

While invidious comparisons are reduced to a minimum, the teacher has the materials for accomplishing all the purposes of ability grouping.

In preparing this series of arithmetic texts, we have had the benefit of much assistance, especially the advice of teachers, supervisors, superintendents, and specialists in arithmetic, and the generous coöperation of the publishers and their editorial staff. We acknowledge this assistance with hearty thanks.

GRACE 

Using Arithmetic in Earning Money 

Have you ever sold things to earn money? Alice and 

Jack are setting up a lemonade stand. They have bought lemons, sugar, and ice. They must sell the lemonade for more money than they have paid out, or they will lose instead of gain. 

While Alice and Jack are getting their lemonade stand ready, they are using arithmetic to find how much money they may earn. Complete their problems on the next page by asking the questions which they ought to ask, and then solve the problems. 1

1. We bought 3 dozen lemons at 45 cents a dozen. 

How much...? 

2. We bought 4 pounds of sugar at 7 cents a pound. 

How much...? we 3. The ice cost 15 cents. All together, how much have · · ? 

4. If 1 lemon will make 2 glasses of lemonade, and we use 3 dozen lemons, how many . . .? 

5. If we sell all the lemonade at 5 cents a glass, how much...?

When they have worked all these problems, they will know how much they have paid out and how much they may take in. Their next question will be: 

6. If we sell all the lemonade, how much . . .? 

But, sales of lemonade may be slow. People may not be thirsty, or the stand may not be attractive, or Jack and

Alice may not be good salesmen. They will ask themselves more problems, like these: 

7. If we sell only 60 glasses of lemonade, how much money shall we take in? How much shall we earn? 

8. If we sell only 50 glasses, how much money shall we take in? How much shall we earn? 

9. If we sell only 40 glasses, how much money shall we take in? How much shall we earn? 

10. If we sell only 30 glasses, shall we gain or lose? 

How much? 

11. How many glasses must we sell so that we shall not lose money?

Making Problems 

3 

How Problems Are Made 

Finish making these problems by asking a good question about each one. 

Then work the problems. 

1. There are 34 children in Miss Warren's class and 26 children in Miss Carter's class. 

2. In Miss Walker's room, there are 5 rows of desks. 

Each row has 7 desks. 

3. Helen has read 140 pages in her new book. There are 326 pages all together in the book. 

4. In the school lunch room, Sam bought a glass of milk for 4 cents, an egg sandwich for 8 cents, and a baked apple for 7 cents. 

5. The fifth grade of the Franklin School ordered 28 copies of the school newspaper. The newspaper costs 2 cents a copy. 

6. There were 254 pupils in the Liberty School. One stormy morning only 210 were present. 

7. The fifth and sixth grades had a race to see which would sell more tickets to the school play. The fifth grade sold 161 tickets and the sixth grade sold 140. 

8. Miss Martin's class was given a strip of the school garden 60 feet long. There were 20 children in the class. 

9. Betty took a dollar bill to the flower store to buy a pot of ivy for the fifth-grade room. It cost 75 cents. 

10. Ann has a spelling list of 40 words to learn. She wants to learn them all in 8 days. 

11. The 32 pupils in our class are going to the park by bus. The fare is 7 cents apiece.

How Problems Are Solved 

When you solve arithmetic problems, you have to add or subtract or multiply or divide. These are the four important processes in arithmetic. Sometimes you have to use two or even three of the processes in order to solve one problem. 

How do you know whether to add or subtract or multiply or divide? It is easy to decide if you know what the problem means. If, for instance, it tells about buying articles in a store, you can imagine that you are doing the buying.

That is a situation that you know about, for you have bought articles in a store many times. You know that when you buy 3 pounds of sugar at 7 cents a pound, you have to multiply; when you buy a loaf of bread, a pound of butter, and a can of tomatoes, you have to add the prices; and when you spend some of your money, you subtract it. The problems in the arithmetic book were in the kitchen or office or shop or somewhere else before they were put into the book. They are about things you have done or seen, or that you know of in some way.

You can do easily each problem on the next page if you use this plan : 

1. Read the problem to find what it is about. 

2. Read it again to learn all that it tells. 

3. Imagine you are in the situation of the problem. 

4. Find just what question the problem asks. 

5. Decide whether you ought to add or subtract or multiply or divide to answer the question. 

6. Then use a pencil if necessary and work the problem.

Solving Problems 

5 Choosing the Process to Fit the Situation She 

1. Sarah wanted to buy a book that cost $1.75. had saved only $1.35. How much more money did she need to buy the book? 

2. A loaf of bread costs 12 cents. How much must I pay for 4 loaves? 

3. Jane weighs 80 pounds. Her brother weighs 67 pounds. How many more pounds does Jane weigh than her brother? 

4. A schoolroom is 12 yards long. How many feet long is it? 

5. Edith had $5.85 in the school savings bank. She put in $1.15 more. How much did she have in the bank then? 

6. Last summer Fred earned $10.00 working for his father. He spent $3.75 for a sweater. How much did he have left? 

7. In a fifth-grade room, there are 18 girls, 16 boys, and the teacher. How many people are there in the room all together? 

8. If you learn to spell 4 new words each school day, and there are 180 school days in the year, how many words do you learn to spell in a year? 

9. Mr. Wood's car can go 20 miles on a gallon of gasoline. 

How many gallons will he need if he is going on a trip of 280 miles? 

10. Sam can take a step 2 feet long. He must take 17 such steps in going from one end of the room to the other. 

How long is the room?

Add or Subtract? 

Should you add to get a larger number, or subtract to find a remainder or difference? Imagine that you are in the situation of the problem. 

1. This week our room saved $2.40 for the savings bank, last week $2.32, and the week before $2.18. How much have we saved for the bank in the last three weeks? 

2. My brother was born in 1919. In what year will he be 21 years old? 

3. Andrew is paying his mother's bill of $1.75 at the store. 

How much change should he receive from two dollars? 

4. It is only 5: 15 o'clock, but Jack is hungry. How long before it will be 6:30 o'clock? 

5. Mrs. Green received 3 bills in one day. The telephone bill was $4.70, the electricity bill was $5.88, and the gas bill was $3.62. What was the total amount of these bills? 

6. The sum of two numbers is 252. One of the numbers is 134. What is the other number? 

7. Mr. Price has a gasoline filling station. On Monday morning there was nothing in his tank. Then he had 1350 gallons put in. During the day he sold 560 gallons. How many gallons were left in the tank? 

8. George says he needs $5.75 more to buy a bicycle which costs $25. How much money has he? 

9. In our school we have 240 boys. This number is 25 more than the number of girls. How many girls have we in our school?

Solving Problems 

7 

Multiply or Divide?

In each of the following problems, you are to multiply or divide. Read each problem, decide what process should be used, and then solve the problem. 

1. I want to buy 3 dozen oranges costing 55¢ a dozen. 

How much will the 3 dozen oranges cost? 

2. Mrs. Evans paid me 75 cents for mowing her lawn. 

It took 3 hours. How much an hour did I earn? 

3. We got a case of 144 eggs from the country. How long will they last if we use 8 eggs each day? 

4. Walter worked 7 days at $1.75 a day. How much did he earn? 

5. How far will a car go in 6 hours if it goes 35 miles an hour? 

6. Will sold 312 papers in a week. He did not work on 

Sunday. If he had sold the same number of papers each day, how many would that be per day? 

7. The fare on a bus is 2 cents a mile. How much will it cost to ride in the bus to my grandfather's home, which is 

25 miles away? 

8. Potatoes are 28 cents a peck. How much will a bushel of potatoes cost at the same rate? 

9. How many weeks are there in 365 days? How many days are left over? 

10. Mary has 120 words to study. If she learns 8 words a day, how long will it take her to learn the 120 words? 

11. Twenty nickels make a dollar. If I have $5 changed to nickels, how many nickels shall I receive? 

12. I have 12 quarters in my pocket. How many dollars is that?

What Must You Do with the Numbers? 

Think: What is the question? What must I do with the numbers to answer it? 

1. The butcher says chickens sell for 34 cents a pound. 

How much must I pay for a chicken that weighs 5 pounds? 

2. The butcher has charged me $2.24 for a roast that weighs 8 pounds. How much per pound is that? 

3. A newsboy sold 48 copies of the Herald and 84 copies of the News. He made 1 cent on each paper sold. How much did he earn? 

4. At 84 cents each, how much will it cost to buy arithmetics for 32 pupils? 

5. Five girls went camping. The expenses for all amounted to $34.60. What was the expense for each girl? 

6. After you have practiced for 35 minutes, how many minutes more must you practice to complete an hour? 

7. Mr. Stone bought a car for $1450, but he could pay only $1080 in cash. He owed the rest. How much did he owe? 

8. Frank sells newspapers for 3 cents each. Today, when he counted the money that he had received for his papers, he had $1.68. How many papers had he sold? 

9. Ruth's grocery bill is $4.62. How much change should she receive from $5? 

10. Gasoline costs 19 cents a gallon. How much must I pay to fill my tank, which holds 15 gallons? 

11. Mr. Ward earns 90¢ an hour. How much does he earn in a day when he works 8 hours?

11 

What Numbers Are Not Needed? 

Read these problems very carefully, for in each there is a number which you do not need to use in solving the problem. 

In each problem tell which number you do not need, and then find the answer to each question. 

1. Jack has 60 hens. One day they laid 3 dozen eggs. 

How much are the eggs worth at 42 cents a dozen? 

2. Mary is planning a party. There will be 15 guests. 

She will need 3 quarts of ice cream. How much will it cost at 55 cents a quart? 

3. John has $1. He is buying a knife for 65 cents and a ticket for the school play for 25 cents. How much is he spending? 

4. Betty is buying a 3-pound can of coffee for $1.05. 

How much change will she get from $2? 

5. An automobile with 4 people in it is going 33 miles an hour. At that rate, how far will it go in 5 hours? 

6. At a special sale I can buy 25-cent handkerchiefs for 

18 cents each. How much will 8 handkerchiefs cost? 

7. At the fruit store I read a sign: "Oranges 50 cents a dozen. 3 dozen for $1.25." I bought 3 dozen oranges and a basket of grapes. The grapes cost 29 cents. How much was my bill? 

8. A grocer bought coffee for 34 cents a can. He is selling it for 45 cents a can. Mrs. Howard is buying 4 cans. 

How much will she pay the grocer? 

9. A basket holding 16 peaches cost 65 cents. Mrs. 

Graham bought 4 baskets. How much did she pay for them?

A Very Old Way of Writing Numbers 

People of long, long ago did not write numbers as we write them. The picture shows how one ancient people, the 

Chaldeans, wrote the numbers from 1 to 12. They wrote them with sticks on clay tablets. ٢٢ YYY 

How do you suppose the Chaldeans wrote 13? 

How do you suppose they wrote 15? 20?

The Roman Way of Writing Numbers 

(1) The Romans, many hundreds of years after the Chaldeans, used another way of writing numbers. 

We use

Roman numbers even today in some places. The Romans used these seven letters for numbers:

I = 1 5 = 10 

50 C = 100 D = 500 M = 1000

The Romans put these letters together to form other numbers in this way:

III = 3 (adding 3 ones) XX 20 (adding 2 tens) 

CCC = 300 (adding 3 hundreds)

Reading and Writing Numbers 

13 When a smaller number was put after a larger number, its value was added to that of the larger number: VI 6 (or 5 + 1) 

XI 11 (or 10+ 1) 

LX 60 (or 50+ 10) 

LXV = 65 (or 50+10+5)

When a smaller number was put before a larger number, the value of the smaller number was subtracted from that of the larger number:

IV == 

4 (or 5 

1) XL 40 (or 50 - 10) 

IX = 9 (or 10 - 1) (2) Write XC in our figures. 

Write CX in our figures. 

Write MC in our figures. 

CM 900 (or 1000 - 100) 

How much is CLX? MDCCC? CCCVII?

XIV is 14 (10+ 4). How much is XIX? XXIV? LIX? 

CXL? (3) The chapters of this book are numbered in the Roman way. How many chapters are there? Have the chapters in any of your other books Roman numbers?

Some watches and clocks have Roman numbers on the faces. Draw a clockface with Roman numbers.

The year in which a church or any large building is built is often carved on the corner stone in Roman numbers. 

The stone may not be at a corner of the building, but it is usually in front. Find a corner stone which is marked with 

Roman numbers. What is its date?

On the front page of a magazine or newspaper, you are likely to find the number of the volume in Roman numbers. 

The abbreviation for volume is Vol. Can you explain what "Vol. XXI" means?

Practice with Roman Numbers 1. The last chapter of my language book is Chapter XIII. 

2. The last chapter of my history is Chapter XXXVI. 

3. The preface and the table of contents of my geography are on pages III to IX. 

4. Our school uses a great deal of paper. The principal received a bill the other day for 5M envelopes. 

5. This volume of the Daily News is Vol. XIV. 

6. The date on the corner stone of our school is MCM. 

7. The corner stone of an old church shows that the church was built in MDCCCXXXVI. 

8. The oldest book in our school library was printed in MDCCCLXV. 

Our Way of Writing Numbers 

(1) We use an easy way of writing numbers. We did not invent it. Our ancestors in Europe learned it from people who lived in Asia. It is called the Arabic way. 

The Romans wrote a certain number MMMCCCXXXIII. 

Is it easy to read this number? We write the same number 

3333. What makes our way so much simpler?

We do not have a special letter or figure for one thousand, another for one hundred, another for ten, and another for one. 

We know that in the number 3333:

The right-hand figure (point to it) means 3. 

The next figure to the left (point to it) means 10 X 3. 

The next figure means 100 × 3 or 300. 

The next figure means 1000 X 3 or 3000.

Reading and Writing Numbers 

15 (2) Often we write numbers in ruled columns. Notice, for example, the following: 

In writing 5764: 

4 goes in the units' column. | 5 | 7 | 6 | 4 

6 goes in the tens' column (because it is 6 tens). 

7 goes in the hundreds' column (because it is 7 hundreds). 

5 goes beyond the heavy line to the thousands' column (because it is 5 thousands). (3) On the blackboard, rule a set of columns in groups of three, like those below, with heavy lines and light lines. 

Write the column and group names, as shown:

HUN- BILLIONS MILLIONS THOUSANDS DREDS TENS UNITS 

Write these numbers in the ruled columns :

14937 60407 125025 2465216 (4) It is easier to read numbers when they are in ruled columns because the column names help. Co 40 1 4 9 3 is 14 thousand, 937. 0 4 0 is 60 thousand, 407.

CO 1 2 5 0 2 5 24 6 is 125 thousand, 25. is 2 million, 465 thousand, 216. 52 1 6 Copy the numbers above. Put commas where the heavy ruled lines would be, to separate the units, tens, and hundreds from the thousands, and to separate the thousands from the millions. The commas make the reading easier.

Adding Columns Quickly

Here are some ways in which you can save yourself time in working the examples : 

1. Copy correctly. 

2. Make figures that you and every one else can read. 

3. Make the columns straight so that you can add quickly. 

4. Remember decimal points and dollar signs. 

5. Keep your mind on the example. 

To check is to prove that your answer is right. To check addition, add again, upward this time.

Solving Problems 

21 

Some Addition Situations 

Here are some problems the Green family had to solve. 

Try to imagine that you are in the situation yourself. 

1. Dick Green's outfit for school cost: suit $12.75, shirts $3.72, cap $.75, and shoes $4.95. How much did the whole outfit cost? 

2. Ellen Green's outfit cost: dresses $19.50, coat $10.98, shoes $4.85, and hat $1.50. How much did her outfit cost? 

3. In July, Ellen began saving money to buy Christmas presents. She saved 45¢ in July, 60¢ in August, $1.05 in 

September, $1 in October, and 85¢ in November. How much money did she have then for buying Christmas presents? 

4. Dick earns spending money in winter by shoveling snow from the walks. Last winter he earned $1 in December, $2.05 in January, $1.10 in February, and 75¢ in March. 

How much did he earn all together? 

5. It took Bobby Green and his father and mother four days to go in their car to visit his grandfather. On these four days, they drove the following distances: 175 miles, 210 miles, 209 miles, and 224 miles. How far is the Greens' home from the grandfather's? 

6. One week John Green spent $1.75 of his school allowance for lunches, 60¢ for a textbook, 20¢ for paper, and 

25¢ for paints. He had 20¢ left. What is his allowance for one week? 

7. In December, Mrs. Green's household bills were: gas $3.95, electricity $5.08, milk $10.45, and groceries. $70.58. How much did she pay in all?

8. Last Christmas, Mr. and Mrs. Green bought these presents for their four children: for John, a pair of skating shoes for $6.75; for Bobby, a sled for $4.50; for Ellen, a pocket camera for $5.25; and for Dick, a radio set for $10. 

How much did they spend for their children's Christmas presents? 

9. Ellen went to market for her mother and bought 3 packages of oatmeal for 29¢, 3 lb. of prunes for 30¢, 3 lb. of coffee for $1.29, 1 doz. eggs for 52¢, and 10 bars of soap for 55¢. How much did she pay the grocer? 

10. When Mr. Green left home in his car on Monday, he bought 10 gal. of gasoline for $2.10. On Tuesday he bought 

5 gal. for $1.05, on Wednesday 9 gal. for $2.07, and on Thursday 10 gal. for $2.30. (a) How many gallons did he buy all together? (b) How much did the gasoline cost?

Do you always add correctly? Or do you make mistakes? There isn't much use in adding if you are not sure of your sums, is there? Do you want to know what kinds of mistakes you make, so that you can learn to become a good adder? This diagnostic test will show you. Each set of examples has a kind of difficulty which the sets before it do not have. If you get wrong answers for the examples in a set, you have not mastered the difficulty which that set contains. 

Your teacher may not expect you to do some of the sets. 

She will tell you how far you are to go.

Solving Problems 

25 

More Addition Situations 

Remember: Imagine yourself in these situations. 

1. When Mary went to a girls' camp last summer, her mother bought her a pair of hiking shoes for $5.75, a pair of knickers for $7.98, a wool middy for $3.45, two pairs of woolen stockings for $3, and a felt hat for $4.25. How much did the whole outfit cost? 

2. Mr. Phillips has had his new car five months. The first month he drove it 986 miles; the second month, 1452 miles; the third month, 1026 miles; the fourth month, 1598 miles; and the fifth month, 875 miles. How far has he driven it in the five months? 

3. Mrs. Brown's grocery bills for five months were as follows: January $95.48, February $92.60, March $87.95, April $96.20, May $85.95. What was her total grocery bill for these months? 

4. Last month Ted's mother received the following bills: groceries $75.80, gas $5.60, electricity $7.98, laundry $12.60, telephone $3.50. Ted added these bills and found it cost his mother $ to keep house last month.

5. When Mrs. King's groceries were delivered, the following bill was sent with them: sugar $1.65, eggs $.72, flour $1.49, oranges $.70, 6 cans of pears $1.74, potatoes $.69, celery $.15. How much did Mrs. King owe the grocer? 

6. Janet went to the store for her mother with the following list: 1 package of oatmeal, 1 lb. of coffee, 6 bananas, 6 bars of soap, 6 oranges. The clerk made this list of charges: 15, 47¢, 256, 42¢, 30¢. How much did he ask 

Janet for? 

7. The shortest distance by train from the City of 

New York to Chicago is 912 miles; from Chicago to Omaha, 493 miles; from Omaha to Denver, 529 miles; from Denver to Salt Lake City, 472 miles; from Salt Lake City to San 

Francisco, 780 miles. Suppose you lived in San Francisco.

How far would you have to ride on the train to go to the City of New York by way of these cities? (This problem will be more interesting if you find these places on a map.) 

8. The winter's coal for the Sargent family was delivered in four truckloads, as follows: 11,650 lb., 9900 lb., 8000 lb., and 4250 lb. How many pounds of coal did the 

Sargent family buy? 

9. Tom, Harry, and Fred went camping for two days.

They took some food from home but also bought these things: bacon 90¢, bread 20¢, condensed milk 15¢, bananas 

18¢. How much did they spend for food? 

10. The boys rode 35 miles on a railroad train, walked 4 miles, rode on a truck for 5 miles, and then had to walk 5 miles farther to the lake where they were going to camp. 

How far was the lake from the railroad? How far had the boys come from home?

Subtracting Whole Numbers 

29 

What Subtraction Means 

You have learned in the lower grades that you subtract: (1) When you take a number away from another number to find the remainder; for example: 

How much is left when you have a quarter and spend 

6 cents? (2) When you find how much larger or smaller one number is than another number; that is, find the difference between the numbers; for example: 

Mary is 10 years old and her brother is 16 years old. 

How much younger is Mary? (3) When you find how much has to be added to a number to make another number; that is, find the difference between the numbers; for example: 

You have $2.25 and wish to buy a fountain pen that costs $3. How much more money must you have? 

Subtracting and Checking (1) Subtract 247 from 936. 

936 Minuend 247 Subtrahend 

689 Remainder or difference 

Subtract as you did in the lower grades.

CHECK 247 689 936 (2) To check an example in subtraction, add the remainder to the subtrahend. The sum should be the minuend. Do you see why?

The short way to check is not to copy the subtrahend and remainder, but to add them up, in the example, to get the minuend. (3) Explain how to subtract in the following examples.

Oakridge and Maplewood Schools 

Do you subtract in each of these problems? Why? 

1. In the Oakridge public school, there are 941 pupils.

Last year there were only 789. How much has the school grown since last year? 

2. The near-by town of Maplewood has 1223 pupils in the public schools. How many more pupils are there in 

Maplewood than in Oakridge? (See Problem 1.) 

3. In one Maplewood school, there are 501 pupils. Only 

425 pupils were there one stormy day. How many were absent? 

4. The people of Maplewood found that it cost $77.26 a year for each child in the public schools. It cost the people of Oakridge $81.04 for each child. Which town paid more for each child? How much more? 

5. The next year the children of Oakridge helped the school by being very careful of books and supplies. The school board, principal, teachers, and pupils all worked together to prevent waste. That year, instead of $81.04 a child, it cost $79.82 a child. 

How much did Oakridge save that year on each child in the public school? 

6. Find the size of your school and find how much larger or smaller it is than the Oakridge school. 

7. Find the school enrollment in your town or city. Is it larger or smaller than the enrollment in Maplewood? How much larger or smaller is it? 

8. Find the cost of keeping a child in the public school for one year in your town or state. Make a subtraction problem, using the cost of the Maplewood school.

Solving Problems 

35 

Some Geography Problems 

Remember to imagine yourself in the situation of each of these problems. 

1. When Douglas went on a trip to Niagara Falls, he was told that the Falls are 167 ft. high. He was surprised to learn that the highest known waterfall in the world, Sutherland Falls in New Zealand, is 1904 ft. high. How much higher is this than Niagara Falls? 

2. Margaret and Eleanor both lived in Chicago. Margaret was going to Springfield, Mass., one summer and 

Eleanor was going to Boston. They found that the distance from Chicago to Boston is 1019 miles, and from 

Chicago to Springfield, 921 miles. How much farther is it from Chicago to Boston than from Chicago to Springfield? 

3. The distance by steamship from Boston to Hamburg, Germany, is 3995 miles. The distance from the City of 

New York to Hamburg is 4201 miles. Which city is nearer 

Hamburg? How much nearer?

4. Charles and Henry live in St. Louis. One day they were talking about how far they had traveled. Charles had come to St. Louis from San Francisco, where he used to live, and Henry had come from Boston. They found that 

Boston was 1217 miles from St. Louis and that San Francisco was 2197 miles from St. Louis. Which boy had come farther? How much farther? 

5. Two of the great rivers that flow into the Mississippi are the Ohio and the Missouri. The Ohio is 968 miles long. 

The Missouri is 2945 miles long. Which river is longer? 

How much longer? 

6. One of the fastest trips around the world was made in about twenty-eight days. The entire distance traveled was 20,100 miles. Of this distance, 11,600 miles were traveled by train, automobile, and boat. The rest of the trip was made by airplane. How many miles were traveled in the air? 

7. Two men who tried to fly all the way around the world had to give up (because of storms and poor landing places) after they had gone 12,295 miles. The entire distance around the world by the way they planned to follow was 

22,212 miles. How much farther did they have to go when they gave up the flight? 

8. Make up and solve a subtraction problem about the lengths of two rivers. 

9. Make up and solve a subtraction problem about the distances between cities. 

10. Make up and solve a subtraction problem about the heights of two mountains.

For What Process Does the Situation Call? 

Imagine yourself in the situation. First read each problem through carefully and tell how to work it. Then with paper and pencil work the problem. 

1. Walter had $37.15 in the savings bank and put in $4.59 more. How much did he have in the bank then? 

2. Henry earned $65 last vacation, and of this he spent $28.48. How much did he save? 

3. Ned bought a secondhand bicycle for $9.75 and, after repairing it, sold it for $12. How much did he receive for his work of repairing it? 

4. John's family took an automobile trip of 524 miles.

After they had gone 276 miles, how many miles did they still have to go? 

5. John's father bought a car for $900. After using it for a year, he sold it for $575. How much less did he receive for it than he had paid? 

6. At a meeting George counted, besides himself and the chairman, 37 boys and 34 girls. How many were at the meeting? 

7. In a school of 954 pupils, 496 are girls. How many boys are there in that school? 

8. Dan's mother gave him $4.50 to buy some groceries. 

He brought back 95¢ change. How much money had he spent? 

9. Mr. Cox was offered an automobile for $825. The owner said that this price was $495 less than the car cost him when new. What did it cost when new?

Solving Problems without Pencil 1. After Agnes had spent $.10 for paper and $.05 for a pencil, she had a dime and a quarter left. How much had she had at first? 

2. In a schoolroom there are 18 boys, 22 girls, and the teacher. How many people are there in the room? 

3. What four different coins together make 41¢? 

4. Dick is 5 years younger than his sister. Dick is 

8 years old. How old is his sister? 

5. Sam had 25 chickens, but one night some were stolen. 

He had 19 left. How many were stolen? 

6. If you bought something for 35¢ and gave the clerk 

50¢, what 2 coins might he give you in change? 

7. If a train due at 6:15 is 20 minutes late, when will it arrive? 

8. Harry was given $2 on his birthday. He spent $1.50 for a watch. How much did he have left? 

9. How many minutes are there from quarter past ten to half past eleven o'clock? 

10. Bob went to the store for his mother. He bought butter for 40¢, eggs for 304, and a box of cookies for 10¢. 

How much did all cost? 

11. Dora has taken 50¢ to the store to buy gloves. But the gloves cost 65¢. How much more money does she need? 

12. On her vacation Mary spent $.30 for post cards, $.25 for stamps, and $.05 for a pad of paper. How much did she spend for these articles? 

13. Mr. Hall is 40 years old. How old was he 13 years ago?

A Zero Difficulty: Multipliers Ending in 0 or 00 (1) Multiply 65 by 70. 65 70 4550 

0 x 650. Write the product 0 under the multiplier figure 0. 

7 × 65. 7 x 5 = 35. Write 5 under the multiplier figure 7. Carry 3. 7 × 6 = 42. 42 + 3 = 45 (2) Multiply 879 by 200. 879 200 175800 In the product, set down O's under the two 0's at the end of the multiplier. Then multiply by 2, placing 

8, the first figure, under 2, the figure you are multiplying by.

What Is the Question? Can You Find the Answer? 

1. Sally is making taffy to sell before Christmas. She will wrap each piece in waxed paper and put 20 pieces in a box. She expects to fill 32 boxes. How many pieces of waxed paper will she need? 

2. If Sally sells her 32 boxes of taffy for 25¢ a box, how much will she take in? 

3. Miss Hart's pupils are going to have an assembly in the auditorium. They will have printed programs. George counted the number of seats in a row and the number of rows in the auditorium. There are 36 seats in a row and 

18 rows. How many programs will they need if they put one on each seat? 

4. The children in our room are going to sell Christmas seals. They think each child can sell 50 cents' worth. 

There are 40 children in the room. That will make how much money to turn in? 

5. In the eighth grade room each pupil will try to sell $1.50 worth of seals. There are 36 pupils in the room. 

How much will that make to turn in? 

6. Allan has the agency for a magazine, which sells for 

5¢ a copy. He has boys working for him. He gave Frank 

65 copies to sell, George 75 copies, Dan 100 copies, and

Albert 80 copies. If the boys sell all their copies, how much money will each boy turn in to Allan? 

7. Robert's father has a chicken hatchery. Robert is helping to fill an incubator with eggs. There are 48 trays in the incubator, and each tray holds 144 eggs. How many eggs does the incubator hold?

Some Multiplication Situations 

Remember: Think of yourself in these situations. 

1. Mr. Stebbins is a rural mail carrier. Each day (except Sunday) he drives from the village out through the country and back again, delivering and collecting mail. 

The distance he goes each day, his route, is 29 miles. How many miles does he drive each week? 

2. Lucy's brother works in the summer to help pay his way through college. Last summer he worked for 10 weeks and was paid $18 a week. How much did he earn in all? 

3. Mrs. Graham bought a crate (32 boxes) of blackberries for 20¢ a box. How much did she pay for the whole crate? (20¢ is $.20.) 

4. What did Mrs. Black pay for 45 lily bulbs at 9¢ apiece? (9¢ is $.09.) 

5. Phil's wages are $23 a week. How much does he earn in 6 months (26 weeks)? In a year (52 weeks)?

8. Mr. Wright's salary is $275 a month. What is it for a year?

Our Boy Scout Troop 

If you do not need the practice on pages 57-58, solve these problems. 

1. There are 19 Boy Scouts in our troop. (a) Each scout bought a uniform for $5.85. How much did the uniforms for the whole troop cost? (b) Each scout bought 2 khaki shirts @ $1.75 (that is, at $1.75 for each shirt). What was the cost of the shirts for the whole troop? 

2. Find the cost of each item in the following list of articles ordered by the Scout Master; then find the total cost of the articles. (a) 19 hats @ $2.25 (b) 19 handkerchiefs @ 45¢ (c) 19 belts @ 38¢ (d) 27 pr. stockings @ $1.65 (e) 9 sweaters @ $3.85 (f) 17 compasses @ 95¢ (g) 7 air rifles @ $4.65 (h) 13 scout axes @ $1.75

3. Jim is earning money to go to a Boy Scout camp next summer. He works in an office on Saturdays. One Saturday he mailed 32 packages. He put 7 two-cent stamps on each package. How much did the stamps cost all together? 

4. Jerry hopes to go to camp too. Before and after school, he is working as a delivery boy for a butcher near his home. (a) He gets 30¢ an hour and works 3 hours each school day. How much does he earn in a school week (5 days)? (b) He works 9 hours on Saturday. How much does he earn in the whole week?

Choosing the Right Process 

Read each problem, decide what process or processes the situation calls for, and then solve the problem. 

1. An empty box weighed 8 lb. When filled with soap, it weighed 133 lb. Find the weight of the soap that was in the box. 

2. A boy bought a secondhand bicycle for $17.60. He spent $7.40 for repairs. What was the total cost of the bicycle and the repairs?

Solving Problems 

59 

3. When one football suit costs $6.75, what will it cost to buy suits for the 11 boys on the team? 

4. Robert sold 16 baskets of apples for 45¢ a basket. 

How much did he receive for all 16 baskets? 

5. The yarn for a sweater cost Jean $2.67. She sold the sweater for $5. Find how much she made on the sweater. 

6. Jim Ball waits on table in a boarding house to earn part of his college expenses. He is given his meals and $1.50 a day. How much money does he earn in a week? 

7. Mildred earns $85 a month. She finds that her board and room and carfare will amount to $47 a month. 

How much will that leave her for other expenses? 

8. Dick's father earns $175 a month. How much does he earn in 1 year and 9 months? 

9. Lucy works in a mail-order house for $18 a week. 

How much can she earn in 6 months (26 weeks)? 

10. Jim's school used 23 tons of coal last winter. How much did the coal cost at $13.75 a ton?

Solving Problems without Pencil 

1. At the fruit store large apples are 4¢ each. How many can I buy with 20¢? 

2. Eight children in a club said they would share equally the cost of fruit for a picnic. It cost $.72. How much was the share of each? 

3. Harry had 36 Christmas cards to sell. His mother said she would buy one quarter of them. How many would she buy? 

4. Bill's classroom has 42 desks arranged in 6 equal rows. 

How many desks are there in each row? 

5. The price of a book is 50¢, but at a sale it will be sold for half price. What will be the sale price? 

6. I have 48 pieces of candy. I want to divide it equally among 8 children. How many pieces of candy will each child receive? 

7. Agnes has an allowance of 35¢ a week. That is how much a day? 

8. Fred received a birthday gift of 45¢. How long will it last if he spends 5¢ a day? 

9. Mary earned a dollar by working 4 hours. How much an hour did she make? 

10. When marbles cost 8¢ a dozen, how many dozen can 

I buy for 40¢? 

11. Oranges cost 40¢ a dozen. I want to buy 3 oranges, or dozen. How much must I pay for 3 oranges? 

12. At the store I can buy 6 pencils for 20¢. I want to buy 3 pencils. How much will they cost? 

13. The baker sold 6 loaves of bread for 54¢. What was the price per loaf?

Remainders in Division 

(1) You have 17 chocolate bars to divide among 4 people. 

How many bars will each person get? 41 4)17 

Four whole bars, of course; but there is 1 bar left over. 

The one bar left over is also divided by 4. 1÷4 may be written. The answer is 4 bars. (2) Alfred rode his bicycle 23 miles in 4 hours. How many miles an hour was that? 

Is there a remainder? Can it be divided? How will you write it in the quotient?

We find that in some division problems the remainder may be divided. It becomes a fraction, with the remainder as the upper number and the divisor as the lower number.

(3) Divide a 22-inch ribbon into badges 5 inches long. 

You have 4 badges and 2 inches left over. You cannot make a 5-inch badge from the 2 inches. You cannot have of a badge. The 2 inches is just remainder. 4 R2 5)22 

What does R stand for? (4) Make up a problem about dividing a class of boys and girls. Can the remainder in such a problem be divided? (5) Make up a problem about inches, in which the remainder can be divided.

Do You Know When to Use Division?

Division is not the correct process for some of the following problems. Read each problem carefully. Think about what you must do to answer the question. Then, and not until then, use your pencil to get the answer. 

1. Margaret works in a store every day except Sunday. 

She earns $24 a week. How much are her wages a day? 

2. Frank was going to his uncle's, 525 miles away. The first day he went 216 miles. How many miles farther did he have to go? 

3. Miss Arnold is paid a salary of $130 a month for her work in the bank. How much is that a year? 

4. A grocer paid $27 for 9 crates of grapefruit. How much was that a crate? 

5. An airplane is going at the rate of 120 miles an hour. 

How far will it go in 4 hours? 

6. A motor bus travels 848 miles in 4 days. How many miles a day is that? 

7. Mr. Baker sold his 5-acre truck garden for $625. 

How much was that per acre? 

8. A man bought a used car for $585 and paid for it in 

9 equal payments. How much did he pay each time?

Some Division Situations 

If you do not need Graded Practice, try these problems. 

1. There are 4 pecks in a bushel. A bushel of potatoes weighs 60 lb. How much does a peck of potatoes weigh? 

2. A fruit dealer pays $22.00 for 8 crates of melons. 

How much are the melons a crate? 

3. A grocer bought 7 barrels of flour for $68.25. What was the price per barrel? 

4. There are 5280 ft. in a mile. In some cities there are 8 blocks in a mile. How many feet are there in a block in such cities? 

5. Jane, Ruth, and shared the cost equally. 

Ellen bought candy for 60¢. 

How much did each pay? They 6. Mr. Whitman is planning to put a garage costing $900 on his lot. How much would he have to save each month for the garage if he wishes to pay for it in six months? 

7. There are 4 quarts in a gallon. A milk company buys milk in large cans from the farmers, puts it in bottles, and sells it by the quart or pint. One day the company sold 1400 quarts. How many gallons was that? 

8. We sold $7.85 worth of tickets for our play. The tickets cost 5 cents each. How many tickets did we sell? 

9. Ned earned $47.20 one summer. He spent of this and saved the rest. How much did he save? 

10. Margaret, Ethel, Helen, and the three Bassett girls agreed to share equally in the cost of a party for Agnes, who was moving away from town. When they counted up the cost of the party, they found it was $7.44. How much should each girl pay?

Multiply or Divide?

Should you multiply to get a larger number, or divide to get a smaller number? 

1. How much will each boy have to pay if 8 boys share the cost of a tent that costs $17.60? 

2. How many 5-pound bags of sugar can be made from a 200-pound sack? 

3. How much will 100 lb. of sugar cost at 6¢ a pound? 

4. A clerk sold 8 arithmetic books for $6.80. What was the price of each book? 

5. If you save 5¢ a week, how long will it take you to save enough to buy a book costing $1.40? 

6. If I save 35¢ each week, how much will I save in a year? (Count 52 weeks to the year.) 

7. Mrs. Bell has 240 jars of jelly to pack in boxes holding 

8 jars each. How many boxes can she fill? 

8. Mother bought a 6-pound chicken for $1.92. How much per pound did the chicken cost? 

9. The manager of the Lowell School basket-ball team divided 135 tickets equally among 5 boys who had offered to sell tickets. How many tickets did each boy receive? 

10. Charles has sold 45 tickets for a school play at 25¢ each. How much money did he collect for these tickets? 

11. Mrs. Harris intends to plant 144 tomato plants in her vegetable garden. If she plants 8 to a row, how many rows will she have? 

If the fare 12. There were 65 passengers on a trolley car. was 5¢, what amount was paid by these passengers?

Divide 252 by 63. 4 63)252 252 

Two-Figure Divisors 

1. Estimate the quotient of 252 ÷ 63. An easy way is to think how many times 6, the first figure of the divisor, is contained in 25. 6 is contained in 25, 4 times. 

2. Write the quotient 4 above the last figure of the dividend. 

3. To find whether 4 is the right quotient, multiply 4 × 63 and write the product under the dividend 252. write 2. Think 4 × 6 = 24, plus 1 = 25. 

Think 4 × 3 and 

Write 25. Draw a line. There is no remainder. 4 is the right quotient. Practice 

Divide. When you multiply to find whether you have the right quotient, use your pencil only to write the product.

Our School Picnic 

1. We are going to have a school picnic at the lake. There are 264 people going to the picnic. Each bus can carry 

44 people. How many buses shall we need? 

2. How many dozen paper plates should we buy so that each of the 264 people at the picnic may have a plate? 

3. We are buying paper napkins in packages of 24 each. 

How many packages should we buy for the 264 people? 

4. The girls of the fifth grade have promised to make 

144 sandwiches for the picnic. They are told that they can get 12 sandwiches from a loaf of sandwich bread. How many loaves should they buy? 

5. The boys of the fifth grade want to buy 132 eggs to be hard cooked for the picnic. How many dozen should they buy?

Using Division in the Air 

1. It is about 720 miles by air route from New York to 

Chicago. How long would it take an airplane flying 90 miles an hour to cover that distance? 

2. An airplane flew 1995 miles in 21 hours. How many miles an hour was that? 

3. An airplane stayed in the air for 336 hours without landing. How many days was that? 

4. When an airplane uses 36 gallons of gasoline in flying 

504 miles, how many miles does it go on a gallon? 

5. It is about 570 miles from Atlanta to Annapolis. A mail plane traveling 95 miles an hour will make the trip in hours.

David's Automobile Trip: Averages 

David Parsons and his family took an automobile trip 

David used what he had learned of division last summer. to find out something about averages. The family drove slowly through towns and near schools and at crossings, but on good roads in the country, they went faster. 

1. The first day David found that they had gone 168 miles in the 8 hours they were driving. What was the average distance per hour? 

2. The next day there were good roads and few towns. 

They drove only 7 hours but went 189 miles. What was their average speed? 

3. David wanted to find out, too, the number of miles they went, on an average, to every gallon of gasoline. After they had gone 120 miles the first day, he saw by the gasoline gauge that they had used 10 gallons. He figured that they had averaged miles on a gallon of gasoline.

Long Division 

83 4. His father said they ought to average more miles to the gallon on the country roads. 

10 gallons in going 160 miles. mileage per gallon of gasoline? 

The next day they used 

What was the average 5. When they reached home, David found that they had driven 1845 miles. They had been gone 9 days. How many miles had they averaged per day? 

6. His father told David that the trip had cost $175.50. 

What was the average cost for each of the 9 days? 3. 

7. There were 5 people who took the trip. Since the trip cost $175.50, what was the average cost for each person? 

8. Use your answer to Problem 6 to find the average cost per day for each person.

Long Division: Watch Your Products 

Divide 2829 by 41. 7 41)2829 287 41)2829 

1. Divide: 282 ÷ 41. (7) 

2. Write 7 in the quotient. (Where?) 

3. Multiply: 7 X 41. (287) 

Here is a difficulty. 287 is larger than 282. You cannot subtract 287 from 282. (The name for 

287 is "product.")

The product is too large. Therefore you know that 28241 is not so much as 7. 

Erase 7 and 287 and start again. 

1. Divide: 282 41. 7 is too large. Try 6. 

2. Write 6 in the quotient. 

3. Multiply: 6 X 41. (246) 

246 

4. Subtract. 369 

5. Bring down. 

Finish the example.

Remember: If the product is too large, the quotient figure is too large. Watch your products!

Hint to Good Thinkers: When the product is much too large, you may save time by trying a quotient figure two less, instead of one less, than the first quotient figure tried.

Divisors Ending in 7, 8, and 9* (1) You have seen that when you divide by the first figure of the divisor, you sometimes fail to get the correct quotient figure. This is so in the following example: 

2544 48 ? 48)2544 288 5 48)2544 240 

When you divide 25 by 4, you get the quotient figure 6, which is too large, and you have to try again. 

But look at the divisor 48. It is nearer 50 than 

40. The best trial divisor, therefore, is 5 instead of 4. 

If you divide 25 by the trial divisor 5, instead of by 

4, you get the right quotient number at once. 

You have added 1 to your trial divisor because 

48 is nearer 50 than 40. 

Finish the example.

Here is a suggestion which may help you when you are working long division examples: 

When the divisor ends in 7, 8, or 9, you will often save time by adding 1 to the first figure of your divisor to get your trial divisor. 

18)846 18 is almost 20; therefore 2 (instead of 1) is probably the best trial divisor. 

39)2457 39 is almost 40; therefore 4 is probably the best trial divisor. 

*NOTE TO THE TEACHER. If desired, the following explanation may be omitted and the examples on pages 87 and 88 worked by the rules already given. The method of using larger trial divisors is applicable in only about 

75 per cent of the cases.

As 29 is almost 30, 3 (instead of 2) is probably the best trial divisor. 

173. Try 5. The remainder 31 is larger than the divisor. So 5 is too small. 

Erase and start again. 

Try 6 as a quotient. Now the remainder, 2, is all right. Finish the example. →2 Remember: If the remainder is too large, the quotient figure is too small.

Practice in Long Division 

Remember these five things about long division : 

1. Write each quotient figure above its dividend figure. 

2. Write all the quotient figures, including 0. 

3. See that no product is too large. 

4. See that no remainder is too large. 

5. If there is a final remainder, write it in the quotient as a fraction.

Are These Division Situations? 

Does each of these problems require division? 

1. Mrs. Wright paid $1.75 for a 25-pound sack of sugar. 

How much a pound did she pay? 

2. A bolt of 16 yards of muslin costs $3.52. How much a yard is that? 

3. How many dozen oranges are there in a box containing 

156 oranges? 

4. What is the cost of 1 pineapple when a dozen cost $3.36? 

5. A case of 24 cans of peaches costs $7.44. How much is that per can? 

6. Dick has a poultry yard. One week when he shipped 

75 dozen eggs to the city, he received in payment a check for $22.50. How much was that per dozen? 

7. Our large car will go 14 miles on a gallon of gasoline. 

How many gallons will it use in going 168 miles? 

8. Our small car will go 18 miles on a gallon of gasoline. 

How many gallons will it use in a trip of 234 miles?

9. A crate of 32 boxes of berries sells for $4.80. How much is that a box? 

10. How long will it take a train to go 1000 miles when it is traveling at the rate of 40 miles an hour?

Can You Solve All These Problems ? 

If you do not need the Graded Practice, try these problems.

Think whether each problem is a division situation or not. 

1. John's father wanted to drive to Niagara Falls with his family. The shortest route was about 1200 miles each way. 

They planned to spend 2 days at Niagara and 12 days going and coming. About how many miles a day would they have to drive in order to get home on time? 

2. A barrel of flour is 196 lb. of flour. How many pounds of flour can be put into a sack that will hold an eighth of a barrel? 

3. A car of coal contains 64,000 lb. How many tons is this? (2000 lb. = 1 ton) 

4. Helen wanted a victrola that cost $75. She found it would take her an entire year to save enough money to buy 

How much did she think she could save each month? it. 

5. One year there were 505 pupils in the public schools of a small city. It cost the city $37,067 to run the schools that year. How much was the cost for each pupil? 

6. It took an English aviator 321 hours of actual flying to fly to Australia and back to England. He flew 28,000 miles. What was his average speed per hour? 

7. It cost the state $467,419 to build a stretch of concrete road 19 miles long. How much did the road cost per mile? 

8. One year there were 21,835 Boy Scouts and 944 scout troops in a certain state. Find the average number in each troop. Give your answer to the nearest whole number.

Bob's Railroad Trip to Chicago

Bob Emmet traveled with his father during the Christmas vacation. It was the first long train trip he had ever taken. Bob was on a class committee to bring to school interesting facts he had learned. On the next page are some arithmetic problems which he brought back after talking with trainmen on his trip.

Bob could solve the problems correctly. Can you? 

Read each problem carefully. Be sure, before you work it, that you have chosen the right process to use. 

1. In a train of 9 coaches, each coach had 2 rows of seats, with 19 seats in each row. Each seat held 2 people. How many people would the whole train seat? 

2. Bob counted 57 people in a railroad coach. At the next station, 13 people got off and 7 got on. At the second stop, 9 got off and 13 got on. How many people were there in the coach then? 

3. The distance from Cleveland to Chicago by train is 

357 miles. How many miles does a conductor travel in making four round trips? (A round trip is a trip to a place and back again.) 

4. The distance from Chicago to Cedar Rapids is 219 miles. Omaha is 218 miles beyond Cedar Rapids. How long is the round trip from Chicago to Omaha, by way of 

Cedar Rapids? 

5. The distance from Chicago to Denver by railroad is 

1035 miles. A fast train makes the trip in about 28 hours. 

What is its average speed per hour? 

6. The fastest train from New Orleans to Chicago makes the trip in 21 hours. The distance is 935 miles. What is the average speed of the train? 

7. By one route, the distance from Chicago to Los Angeles is 2354 miles. What is the average speed of a train that makes the trip in 63 hours? 

8. At 3¢ a mile, how much did Bob's father pay for one ticket from Cleveland to Chicago (357 miles)?

A Mastery Test in Division 

97 

A MASTERY TEST IN DIVISION 

Do you know an especially skilful carpenter or farmer or automobile mechanic, one who understands his work and can do every part of it easily, quickly, and without mistakes? He is a master of his trade or profession. Or do you know an unusually able nurse or dressmaker who knows just what to do and can do it without errors? She is a master of her profession. It takes study and much practice to become a master. But the pleasure in being a master pays for all the time and effort. 

Are you a master of division? This mastery test will tell you. Or is there still something about division that you have not fully learned? The mastery test will show you. 

Do your best. Do not be careless. Read carefully. 

Not all the problems are division problems. If you know how to do a problem, do it right. If you do not know how to do a problem, go to the next. Do well what you do. 

Do not divide unless you are sure it is a division situation. 

1. A car goes 18 miles on a gallon of gasoline. How many miles will it go on 360 gallons? 

2. When cans of corn are packed 24 to a case, how many cases will be needed to hold 960 cans? 

3. A case of 24 cans of corn costs $3.95. How much is that per can? 

4. When you know that 48 times a number is 1152, what number have you in mind? 

5. A graduating class of 86 pupils bought a picture for the school. It cost $42.14. How much was each pupil's share of the cost?

6. At $14 a ton, what will a winter's supply of 98 tons of coal for a school cost? 

7. When you drive at an average speed of 27 miles an hour, how long will it take to drive 1107 miles? 

8. Mr. Carter was paying for his house by the month. 

He owed $3610. He paid $38 a month. In how many months would he pay what he owed? 

9. Mr. Sinclair has driven his car 4896 miles in the 

12 months he has had it. What is his average number of miles per month? 

10. There are 16 ounces in a pound. How many pounds are the same as 4800 ounces? 

11. Twenty boys worked for a week to earn money for their club. In all, they earned and turned in $40.07. How much was that for each boy, on the average?

CHAPTER III SOLVING PROBLEMS Choosing the Right Process 

When you read a problem, imagine that you are doing or seeing what the problem tells about. Put yourself in the situation of the problem. Think whether it is a situation in which you would add, subtract, multiply, or divide. 

1. Tom wants to earn a prize by selling 75 magazines for 

15 cents each. How much money will he take in if he sells them all? 

2. Robert wants to buy a sweater he has seen in the store window. The price is $5. He has only $3.95. How much more money does he need? 

3. Arthur can earn $1.75 each Saturday by delivering groceries. At that rate, how much will he earn in a year of 52 weeks? 

4. Agnes has earned $8.20. She wants to spend of it for a gift for her mother. How much money does she want to spend for the gift? 

5. Peggy saw a crate of eggs in the store. The man said that there were 30 dozen eggs in the crate. How many eggs was that? 

6. How many quarters make $5? 

7. How many inches are there in 18 feet? 99

Will he have enough money to buy a sweater for $3.47 and a cap for $1.29? 

10. One afternoon 36 pupils of the Hill School went on a picnic. The committee paid $16.20 for lemonade, cakes, and the rent of the bus. How much was the share of each pupil? 

11. Peggy wondered how many eggs would weigh a pound. She found that 9 eggs weigh about a pound. 

About how many pounds do 36 eggs weigh?

Using Large Numbers 

101 

Problems with Large Numbers

Sometimes you do not see at once how to solve a problem because the numbers are large. If you think of a problem of the same kind, but with easy numbers, you will see how to solve both problems. 

1. The fair lasted 4 days. On the first day 2341 people attended, on the second day 2507 people, on the third day 

2890 people, and on the fourth day 2768 people. How many people attended the fair? 

2. In the morning, when Mr. Edwards opened his store, he had $141.63 in the cash drawer. When he closed the store at the end of the day, he had $480.32. He had not paid out any money during the day. How much money had he taken in during the day? 

3. A farmer sold 36 crates of strawberries for $3.25 a crate. How much did he receive in all? 

4. A farmer sold 45 crates of strawberries for $123.75. 

How much did he receive for each crate? 

5. Ned read that, on the average, 4260 passengers rode in the Green Line buses every day. At that rate, how many passengers will ride in those buses in March? 

6. In May the Davis store sold $4272.58 worth of groceries. The store was open 26 days. How much did the store sell each day, on the average? 

7. A furniture factory burns about 285 tons of coal each year. What does it cost at $5.75 a ton? 

8. The Washington School burned 38 tons of coal last year. The coal cost $332.50. How much per ton did it cost?

Solving Problems A Problem May Have an Unnecessary Number 

Be very careful in reading these problems, for in each problem there is a number which you do not need to use. 

Solve the problems. 

1. The Wilson store is having a sale of 75-cent books for 

50¢. How much will 6 books cost? 

2. There are 30 children in Miss Fay's class. Twentythree of them each want a bottle of milk. How much will the milk cost at 4¢ a bottle? 

3. In 4 days Will did 180 examples. Fifteen of them were wrong and the rest were right. How many examples were right? 

4. Jack has saved $18.60 in 10 weeks to buy a bicycle which costs $22.50. How much more money does he need? 

5. Oranges are 9¢ each, or 3 for a quarter. I want 

2 oranges. How much will they cost? 

6. Mr. White loaded his truck with 8 crates of chickens to take to the city to sell. He sold the chickens for 28¢ a pound. In all, the chickens weighed 276 pounds. How much money did he receive? 

7. Miss Gage has a store. In 1929 her profit was $2740.16; in 1930 her profit was $2318.90; in 1931 her profit was $2800.40. How much more was her profit in 

1931 than her profit in 1929? 

8. Mr. Scott, the grocer, bought 800 pounds of sugar.

It came in 8 sacks and cost $48 in all. How much per pound did it cost?

What Else Do You Need to Know? 

John has 32¢. He wants to go to the circus. How much more money does he need to buy a ticket?

You need to know how much a ticket to the circus costs before you can solve the problem. 

Write what more you need to know to solve each problem. 

1. Alfred bought some bananas. He gave the clerk half a dollar. How much change should he have received? 

2. The boys on a team decided to buy uniforms. All the uniforms cost $54. How much did each cost? 

3. A bag of sugar cost 40¢. How much per pound did the sugar cost? 

4. Miss Chase's class went on a picnic. It cost $8.90.

If the children shared the cost equally, how much did each pay? 

5. Jack sold 120 newspapers for 3¢ each. How much profit did he make? 

6. At my party I want to serve ice cream to 20 people. 

How many quarts of ice cream should I have? 

7. How many rows of chairs were needed to seat 280 children? 

8. How many hours is it from 10 P.M. until 10 o'clock the next day? 

9. Elizabeth lives 4 blocks from the school and Jane lives 5 blocks from the school. How many blocks is it from 

Elizabeth's house to Jane's house? 

10. I have bought a box of large red apples for $2.90. 

Can I make a profit if I sell them for 4¢ each?

Solving Problems What Is the Question? What Must I Do to Find the 

Answer?

Walter wants to buy a toy locomotive for 90¢ and 4 cars for 20¢ each. How much does the train cost? 

1. What is the question? 

What does the train cost? 

2. What must I do to find the answer?

Add the cost of the locomotive and the cost of the cars.

As the problem tells how much 1 car costs, I must first find how much 4 cars cost. $.20 $.80 The cars cost $.80. 

Now I can find the cost of the locomotive and the cars. $.90 .80 $1.70 The train costs $1.70.

Think What is the question? 

What must I do to find the answer? 

What must I find first? 

What must I find next? 

Then I can find the answer.

16. In her supply closet a teacher of the Franklin School has 1 dozen red pencils, 3 dozen black pencils, and 6 blue pencils. If the black pencils are valued at 1¢ each and the red and blue pencils at 3¢ each, what is the value of these supplies? 

17. The train fare to my grandfather's home is $5.50 for grown people. Children from 5 years to 12 years old pay one half as much. I am 10 years old. How much will be the train fare for my father, my mother, and me when we go to visit my grandfather? 

18. How much change shall I receive from $5.00 when I buy dozen oranges at 50¢ a dozen and 6 cans of beans at 

18¢ a can? 

19. Ned and Frank help their father on the farm. Last week they sold a crate of 36 dozen eggs for 32¢ a dozen, but this week eggs have gone up. They sold a crate of 36 dozen eggs for 35¢ a dozen. How much more did they receive for their eggs this week than last week?

Reading and Thinking Problems 

Read a problem until you know what it tells and asks. 

Put yourself in the situation. Think: "What is the question the problem asks? What must I do to find the answer? 

What must I do first? What must I do next? Is there anything else to do to find the answer? 

1. Find the change from a one-dollar bill after buying five articles for 13¢, 20¢, 74, 18¢, and 12¢. 

2. One summer Ned put into the bank $5, $3.25, $12, and $10.80. Later he took out $1.75, $4.48, and $3.19. 

How much did he have left in the bank? 

3. The children in a school wished to raise $50 to fit out the playground. By giving a play, they earned $23.65; by selling candy, $18.75. How much did they still need? 

4. John earned as errand boy $.40 on Monday, $.85 on 

Tuesday, and $1.35 on Wednesday. He bought a book for $.45 and a purse for $.98. How much did he have left? 

5. Mrs. Waters bought some groceries for $12.08. She gave the salesman a $10 bill and a $5 bill. She received $3.92 in change. Was that the right amount of change? 

6. Mrs. Scott had a crate of 150 oranges. She gave one friend 15 oranges, another 18, and another 12. She threw away 8 that were spoiled. How many did she have left? 

7. Robert saves 35 cents a week, Mary 50 cents a week, and little Jane 8 cents. In a year (52 weeks) Mary will save $. more than Robert and Jane together. (Can you do this in two different ways and get the same answer?)

Solving Problems

Finding Averages 

If you spent 25¢ for lunch on Monday, 28¢ on Tuesday, 18¢ on Wednesday, 21¢ on Thursday, and 28¢ on Friday, what was the average amount you spent for lunch that week?

This means: How much money would you have spent each day if you had divided the total amount into 5 equal parts? 

To find the average amount, add 25, 28, 18, 21, and 28, and divide the sum by 5, because you added 5 amounts. 

In solving each of these problems, you must use two processes, addition and division. Tell what numbers you add and what number you divide by in each problem. 

Then work the problems. 

1. Joe sells newspapers after school. During one week he sold 12 papers on Monday, 17 papers on Tuesday, 15 on Wednesday, 18 on Thursday, and 23 on Friday.

Find the average number of papers he sold each day that week. 

2. One month Mary took four tests in spelling. She got 80 in the first test, 93 in the second test, 88 in the third, and 95 in the fourth. Find her average grade. 

3. Each girl on the playground was allowed three trials at throwing the basket ball. Jane threw the basket ball 

45 ft. the first time, 41 ft. the second time, and 46 ft. the third time. Find the average length of her throws. 

4. One week the number of children present in our room by days was: 27, 31, 29, 30, and 28. Find the average daily attendance for the week.

The exercises will help you because : 

1. Doing examples of many kinds will make you watchful. 

2. You will learn to recall quickly just what to do in each process. 

3. You will improve in accuracy, speed, and retention. 

Work for 20 minutes. Be accurate. It is better to do correctly every example you try than to do them all and have some wrong. Be neat and orderly. Always prove or check your work.

You have seen that you sometimes have to use more than one process in solving a problem.

Each of the following problems is solved by using more than one process. 

Think: "What is the question? What must I do to find the answer? What must I find first? Do I add, subtract, multiply, or divide to find it? What must I find next? 

What process do I use to find it? What else must I do to answer the question which the problem asks?"

CHAPTER IV ADDING AND SUBTRACTING FRACTIONS 

You learned something about fractions before you were old enough to go to school. You divided an apple or a slice of bread into 2 equal pieces, and you knew each piece was one half.

When you were older, you could divide 8 pennies or 

16 marbles or 12 nickels into 4 equal parts, and you called each part "one fourth."

You could also divide a sheet of paper into thirds or quarters or eighths. 

You also learned that a pint is as much as a quart, a foot is as much as a yard, and 10 cents is as much as 

50 cents.

​A Fraction Vocabulary 

(1) The words you use when you talk or write about fractions make your fraction vocabulary. It (2) The upper figure of a fraction is the numerator. tells how many halves or thirds or quarters or fifths there are in the fraction. (3) The lower figure of a fraction is the denominator. It tells the kind of fraction. It tells that the fraction is halves or thirds or quarters or fifths. 

When you say, " of the pie is left," 5 (the numerator) tells how many pieces are left, and 6 (the denominator) tells that each piece is a sixth of the pie. (4) The numerator and denominator are the terms of the fraction.

The terms of the fraction are 3 and 8. 

Which term is 3? 

Which term is 8?

(5) A whole number and a fraction, like 1½ or 31, make a mixed number. 

Write two more mixed numbers. (6) A fraction that is less than a whole, like or or 7, is a proper fraction.

A fraction that is equal to a whole or to more than a whole, like 2 or 3 or §, is an improper fraction. 

Improper fractions like 3, §, and 10 may be thought of as top-heavy fractions. Why?

Write an improper fraction that is equal to a whole number. Write an improper fraction that is equal to more than a whole number. (7) Fractions that have the same value, like and 2, or and, are equivalent fractions. 

Write another fraction equivalent to ( 

Write a fraction equivalent to . 8) (8) Fractions that are changed to equivalent fractions with larger numerators and denominators are changed to higher terms. Thus, when is changed to g and they are changed to higher terms. 

Change to sixths (). Change to higher terms. to 12, (9) Fractions that are changed to equivalent fractions with smaller numerators and denominators are changed to lower terms. Thus, when & is changed to and to, 2 12 they are changed to lower terms. 

Change to lower terms. Change to lower terms. (10) Fractions that have the same denominators are similar fractions. Thus, &, are similar fractions.

Another Way to Change Fractions 

(1) You found by studying the circle divided into twelfths that. How could you have found this without looking at a divided circle? 

The denominator 2 must be multiplied by 6 to get 12; therefore, the numerator also must be multiplied by 6. To change to twelfths, multiply both terms by 6; thus: 1 X 6 2 X 6 12

To change to twelfths, multiply both numerator and denominator by 4; thus: 2 X 4 

3 X 4 12 

To change to sixteenths, by what number must you multiply both 3 and 8?

Think: 

3 X ? 

8 X? 16 3 X 2 8X2 6 16 To change a fraction to higher terms, multiply both numerator and denominator by the same number. (2) How do you change a fraction to lower terms? 

To change to lower terms, divide 8 and 12 by either 2 or 4. 

You must divide both numerator and denominator by the same number. Think what number is contained in both 

8 and 12 without a remainder. Either 2 or 4 will do.

Change 8÷2 12 ÷ 2 4 6 to lower terms. 8 ÷ 4 12 ÷ 4 213 

What number is contained in both 10 and 15? 10 ÷ 5 

15 ÷ 5 2 3 99 To reduce to lower terms means to lower terms. change" to

To reduce a fraction to lowest terms, divide both numerator and denominator by the largest number that is contained in both, or continue to divide as long as you can find a number that is contained in both.

Least Common Denominators 

141 Finding Least Common Denominators 

Add & and §. 

A common denominator is 8 x 6, or 48. But can't you think of a smaller number that will contain both 8 and 6? 

Suggestion: If the larger denominator will not contain the smaller denominator without a remainder, try twice the larger denominator, then three times the larger denominator, and so on, until you find the smallest number that will contain both denominators. To find the smallest number that will contain 8 and 6 without remainder, think: 

8? 8 won't contain 6. 16? 16 won't do. 24?

Yes, 24 will. Therefore 24 is the smallest number that contains 8 and 6.

The least common denominator of and is 24.

The Watsons' New Home 

Imagine yourself in these situations. Think what processes to use. Then solve the problems.

The Watsons bought a house last month, and they have been putting it in order. 

1. It took 2 gal. of paint to paint the garage, 1 gal. to paint the henhouse, and 171 gal. to paint the house. 

How many gallons of paint did they use in all? 

2. It took 1 yd. and 23 yd. of silk for curtains for the hall. How many yards of silk did they use?

3. The Watsons had two coats of plaster put on their kitchen. The first coat was in. thick and the last coat was in. thick. How thick were both coats together? 

4. It took 27 yd. and 14 yd. of muslin for curtains for the kitchen. How much muslin did Mrs. Watson buy? 

5. The painter worked 74 hr. one day, 61 hr. the next day, 72 hr. the third day, and 3 hr. the fourth day. How many hours did he work in all? 

6. It took 10 lb. of nails for the frame of the new porch, 6 lb. for the floor, and 4 lb. for the ceiling. How many pounds of nails did it take for the whole porch? 

7. The plasterer worked on the kitchen 7 hr. one day, 31 hr. the next day, 4 hr. another day, and 1 hr. the last day. What did his work cost at $1.20 an hour? 

8. It took 71 yd. of carpet for the lower hall; one strip of 67 yd. and one of 33 yd. for the upper hall; and 98 yd. for the stairs. How many yards was that all together?

149 

Some Addition and Subtraction Situations 

Before you use your pencil, read the problem so carefully that you will know whether to add or subtract. Think:

Should the answer be more or less than the largest number given in the problem? 

1. A year ago Helen weighed 68 lb. Today she weighs 

75 lb. How many pounds has she gained during the year? 

2. John lives 14 miles from the township school. Roger lives 1 miles beyond John's house. How far is it from 

Roger's house to the school? 

3. Dan caught a trout 8 inches long. He boasted about it until he met Jerry, who had caught a trout 9 inches long. 

How much longer was Jerry's trout? 

4. Jerry was sure his trout would weigh at least half a pound more than Dan's. They weighed the fish and found 

Jerry's weighed 1 lb. and Dan's weighed 11 lb. Was 

Jerry right or wrong? 

5. Our troop of Boy Scouts took a hike. We walked 

3 miles the first hour, 34 miles the second hour, and 33 miles the third. How far did we walk in the three hours? 

6. Bob had one package weighing 12 lb. and one weighing 9 lb. How much did the two packages weigh? 

7. Polly weighed 693 lb. on her eleventh birthday. The doctor told her that a girl as tall as she was should weigh 

6ğ lb. more than that. How much should she weigh? 

8. Willis can run the quarter mile in 2 minutes. James can run the same distance in 12 minutes. How many minutes faster can James run the quarter mile?

9. Mary had a heavy package of groceries to carry home. 

She began to wonder just how heavy it really was. she thought I have 3 lb. of sugar, lb. of butter, cheese, 2 lb. of prunes, 3 lb. of potatoes, and lb. of bacon. 

How much did Mary's package weigh, not counting paper and string? 

10. Dick's brother is in the high school. Monday night he studied 14 hours, Tuesday hour, Wednesday 14 hours, Thursday 13 hours, and Friday hour. How many hours did he study at home that week? 

11. Julia's mother bought four pieces of gingham at a special sale yesterday. One piece was 37 yd. long, the second piece 34 yd. long, the third piece 42 yd. long, and the fourth piece 21 yd. long. How many yards of gingham did she buy all together?

Choosing the Process 

If you do not need the practice on pages 153 and 154, try these problems. 

1. Three boys bought a catcher's mitt. Edgar gave of the money, Dick gave of it, and John paid for the rest. 

What part of the cost did John pay?

The whole cost of the mitt is 1 whole. Edgar and Dick paid +1, or 2, of the cost. So John paid the difference: 1 12. 

2. In the Hamilton School, of the pupils are boys. 

What part of the pupils are girls? 

3. Henry lives in the country. His father is taking him to the city Saturday and is letting him plan what they will do. Henry finds they will have 6 hours in town. He wants to go to the theater, which will take 2 hours. He allows 1 hour for lunch and hour for getting from one place to another. The rest of the time he wants to spend at the museum. How long will they have at the museum? 

4. Joe planned to spend of his allowance for clothes, for pleasure, for church, and to save the rest. What part of his allowance was he planning to save? 

5. A grocer bought a 50-gallon barrel of molasses and made the following sales from it: 12 gal., 21 gal., 24 gal., and 5 gal. How many gallons were left in the barrel? 

6. Robert spends of each day in school and in study at home, at meals, and at play. What part of the day is left? 

7. Edith's brother plans to spend of his money for board, for clothes, and for all other expenses, and to save the rest. What part of his money will he save?
