CHAPTER V MEASURES We Measure Almost Everything 

Can you think of something in your house that has not been measured? There are some things, of course, but you cannot think of many. Even the baby's weight has been measured.

People of long ago could not measure accurately, but they could do better than the Indians, who had no clocks or foot rules or quart measures or scales. The Indians told of something that happened two sleeps ago or three moons ago; they said a place was two days' journey away; they measured corn by handfuls and liquids by gourdfuls. 

Not very accurate measures, were they? We have clocks and watches that measure time in seconds, minutes, and hours, and the clocks have to keep the same time, or else someone is late for school. We have foot rules and yard rules that are the same length in every store and every school; our pint and quart measures are the same in Maine and in California; a pound is a pound everywhere. What might happen if an inch were different in Boston and St. 

Louis, or if an hour in Washington were longer or shorter than an hour in Florida? 155

Measures 

We measure things in different ways: one way for time, one for length or distance, one for area, one for weight, one for liquids, and one for such things as berries and apples.

Think of ten things the farmer has to measure. What has to be measured in the kitchen? 

Remember that we measure weight when we use the scales. 

What does the butcher measure? The grocer? The drygoods man? The druggist? 

The hardware-store man? The printer? The teacher? The baker? Your mother? Your father? Who has to measure more carefully, the watchmaker or the carpenter? The dry-goods man or the maker of an automobile engine? 

Liquid and Dry Measures 

We use pints and quarts for measuring liquids like milk and molasses. We use pints and quarts, too, for measuring dry" things like raspberries and strawberries. 66 

What measure do we use for liquids that is not used in dry measure?

What two dry measures do we have that are not used for liquids? 

Name a liquid usually sold by the gallon. Name one sold by the pint. Name one sold by the quart. 

Name three things often sold by the bushel.

Liquid and Dry Measures 

157 

2 pt. 1 qt.

Surface Measures 

(1) A rectangle is a figure with four sides and four square corners. 

A window is a rectangle. What are some other rectangles in your schoolroom? You should see at least twenty.

A square is a rectangle with all four sides equal. 

Are there any squares in your schoolroom? Name them. Are they as common as the long kind of rectangle?

A square inch is a square, each side of which is 1 inch long. 

A square foot is a square, each side of which is 1 foot long. 

What is a square yard? (2) Draw a square inch, a square foot, and a square yard on the blackboard.

Measures 

In measuring surfaces like sheets of paper, you use the square inch. What other surfaces would you measure in square inches? What surfaces in your schoolroom would you measure in square feet or square yards? (3) Cut a square of paper with each side 2 in. long. Fold it in square inches. How many square inches are there in the 2-inch square? (4) Draw a rectangle 4 in. long and 2 in. wide. With your square inch of folded paper, find how many square inches will cover it. 

Did you find that it took 2 rows of square inches, with 

4 square inches in each row? Draw lines to show the square inches on your rectangle. You see that this rectangle has 

2 times 4 square inches, or 8 square inches (8 sq. in.). (5) Betty made a pan of fudge 8 in. long and 6 in. wide. She cut the fudge into squares each 1 in. long and 1 in. wide. 

How many squares of fudge did she have? 

She had 6 X 8 squares, or sq. in. (6) Cut a square foot out of wrapping paper.

Draw on the board a rectangle 3 ft. long and 2 ft. wide. 

With your square foot of paper, find how many square feet there are in this rectangle. What is a shorter way to find this? Think of the rectangle divided into rows of square feet. There are 2 X 3 square feet, or square feet.

The number of square inches or square feet in a surface is called the area of the surface. 

You see that you can find the area of the surface of a rectangle by multiplying the length number by the width number.

Cancellation 177 

Saving Work: Cancellation 

When you multiply fractions, you often have to reduce the product to lower terms. Sometimes you may do a part of the reducing before you multiply, and thus save yourself work. 

In Example (1) below, consider the whole number, 3, as the numerator of a fraction with the denominator 1. Then 

X3 may be expressed as X. Now you may divide both the 3 in the numerator and the 9 in the denominator by 3 before you multiply. 

This process is called cancellation.

WITHOUT CANCELLATION 

(1) X 3 

6 2 9 3 

USING CANCELLATION 

2/13 2 13/1 213 (2) 12 X 516 

60 = 10 5181 10 = 10 1 

Remember that in cancellation: 

1. You divide one numerator and one denominator by the same number. 

2. In multiplying a whole number and a fraction, the whole number is always considered a numerator with the denominator 1. 3 

3. In examples like of 6 or 2 of 16, " of " means X or "times." Therefore of 6 means X 6.

Estimating Answers 

Before working an example, you can often tell nearly what the answer will be. This is called "estimating an answer."

Then, after you have worked the example, you can compare your answer with the estimate, to see whether your work is reasonable. For instance, notice how you would estimate the following example:

What will be the cost of 3 pairs of shoes which sell for $4.86 a pair?

Since $4.86 is a little less than $5, you can estimate the answer at a little less than 3 × $5, or $15. 

When you multiply 3 × $4.86, you find that the answer is $14.58, which is close to the estimate of $15.

Are These X or Situations?

In the following problems, you will need to use either multiplication or division. To decide which process to use, think how you would solve the problem if you had whole numbers to work with instead of fractions. Thus, in the first problem, if the napkins were 2 yd. long and 6 napkins were needed, you see that you would multiply to find the answer.

FRACTIONS NOTE TO THE TEACHER: Consult the Manual for directions for giving and using these diagnostic tests. 

What is it that you do not know? If you have trouble in multiplying and dividing fractions, this test will show just what you have failed to learn.

Diagnostic Test in Fractions 

203 Each set of examples in the test has a kind of difficulty which the sets before it do not have. If you get wrong answers for the examples in a set, you have not mastered the difficulty which that set contains. 

Your teacher may not expect you to do all the sets. She will assign the sets which she thinks you ought to do without difficulty. 

Compare your answers with those on page 206. Try to find your errors. Get help on what you do not understand. 

Learn the right way. Then practice by doing the examples in the corresponding sets on pages 206-207.

Grouping to Save Work 

If you do not need the graded practice on pages 206-207, try these problems. (1) When 3 lemons cost 10¢, what will a dozen lemons cost? 

You may think of each 3 lemons as a group. Then a dozen (12) lemons will make 4 groups of 3 lemons each.

Therefore a dozen lemons will cost 4 times as much as 3 lemons. 

4 × 10¢ 40¢ (2) The sign on a pile of oranges was: "4 for 25¢.” 

What would a dozen cost? 

124 3. Then 12 oranges are 3 times as many as 4 oranges. 

3 X 25¢ = 75¢. Therefore 12 oranges would cost 3 X 

25¢, or 75¢. (3) Grapefruit sell at 3 for 25¢. How many grapefruit can I buy for 50¢? 50¢ is 2 times 25¢. Then for 50¢, I can buy 2 × 3, or 6 grapefruit.

Finding What Part One Number Is of Another Number 

A foot is what part of a yard? (Show by lines on the board.) 

An inch is what part of a foot? (Show by a ruler.) 

Ten cents is what part of 50 cents? (8 or ) 

Three cents is what part of 21 cents? 

Six is what part of 7? (Show by a line divided into 

7 parts.) 

Ten minutes is what part of an hour? 

Two days is what part of a week?

To find what part one number is of another number, write the two numbers as a fraction : 

1. Use the number after "of " as denominator. 

2. Use the other number as numerator.

Using Fractional Parts in Problems 

(1) When 20 acres of land are worth $3000, how much will 10 acres of this land be worth? 

20 acres are worth $3000. 

1 acre is worth $3000 ÷ 20, or $150. 

10 acres will be worth 10 × $150, or $1500. 

This problem may be worked in another way: 

10 acres are 10, or, of 20 acres. 

10 acres will be worth of $3000, or $1500. (2) Paul and Dick bought a collection of 50 postage stamps for 10¢. Paul paid 7¢ and Dick paid 34. How many stamps should Paul have? 

Paul paid of the money. 

He should have +7% of 50 = of the stamps.

CHAPTER VII DECIMALS Common Fractions and Decimal Fractions: Tenths 

(1) The fractions you studied in the last chapter are called common fractions, because they have been more commonly used in the past. But decimal fractions are now used almost as much as common fractions. 

You already know something about decimal fractions. 

You have used them to write the cents' part of money numbers. In $18.75, the decimal point (the dot) shows where the whole dollar number ends, and the fraction dollar number begins. 

What number would $18.75 be without the decimal point? (2) All four of the following sentences tell the same fact about the cake. Which tells it in the shortest way?

One tenth of this cake has been cut out. 

1 tenth of this cake has been cut out. of this cake has been cut out. .1 of this cake has been cut out. .1 is a short way of writing 10. .1 is a decimal fraction. (3) How many tenths of these matches have been lighted?- Write the answer as a decimal fraction and then as a common fraction. 215

Decimals (4) Copy these sentences and fill in the blanks with the decimal fractions: 

of these marbles are colored. of these marbles are white. (5) Learn the meaning of these terms: 

1 is a common fraction. .3 is a decimal fraction. 

5 is a mixed number. 

5.3 is a mixed decimal number.

But usually, by decimal, we mean either a decimal fraction or a mixed decimal number.

Using Addition of Decimals in Problems 

1. In four years, Walter gained in height 2.4 in., 1.8 in., 1.6 in., and 1 in. How many inches did he gain in all? 

2. It is 107.5 miles from Jim's home to Springfield. 

His uncle's home is 5.7 miles the other side of Springfield. 

How far is it from Jim's home to his uncle's? 

3. During a three-day storm, the river rose 4.72 in. one day, 2.52 in. the next day, and 3.7 in. the next day. 

How much did the river rise during the storm? Tell in a whole number about how many inches this was. 

4. The rainfall during March was 3.72 in., during April 

2.16 in., and during May 1.3 in. What was the total rainfall for these three spring months? 

5. David rode his bicycle 18.7 miles Monday, 13.9 miles 

Tuesday, and 9.4 miles Thursday. How far did he ride in the three days? 

6. Four loads of hay were put into a barn. The first load weighed 1.48 tons, the second 1.6 tons, the third 1.75 tons, and the fourth 2.15 tons. Find the total weight of the hay. This is almost tons. (Use a whole number.) 

7. One week on different days, Tom walked 1.5 miles, 3.25 miles, 275 miles, 4 miles, 3.33 miles, and 7.6 miles. 

How many miles did he walk in all? 

8. The distance from Jennie's house to the station is 1.37 miles; from the station to Milltown, it is 13.9 miles; and from Milltown to Waterville, it is 9.7 miles. How far does 

Jennie travel in going from her house to Waterville and back?

It is 21.25 miles from Randolph to Northfield. (3) How much change will you receive from a five-dollar bill when you pay a bill for $1.75? $5.00 1.75 $3.25 

Express $5 as $5.00, by adding a decimal point and two decimal O's. 

You will receive $3.25 in change.

Decimals 

Multiplying Decimals (1) At the rate of $1.25 a bushel, what will 9 bushels of potatoes cost? $ 1.25 $11.25 

You have multiplied money numbers before. Where did you put the decimal point in the answer? $1.25 has two decimal places in it. That means it has two figures after the decimal point. The product must have two decimal places in it. Therefore we count off the 5 and the 2, and write the decimal point. 

The potatoes will cost $11.25. (2) How far can a troop of Boy Scouts hike in 3.5 hours at the rate of 2.5 miles an hour? 2.5 3.5 1 25 75 8.75

The multiplicand has one decimal place. The multiplier has one decimal place.

When both multiplicand and multiplier are decimals, the product must have as many decimal places as multiplicand and multiplier together.

The product in this example must have two decimal places. Begin at the right to count them. 

In 3.5 hours the boys can hike 8.75 miles. 

You will get the same answer if you multiply the common fraction way: 

3150 × 215% = 35 × 25 = 975 = 8,75 3.5 X 2.5 = 8.75 = 8.75 (3) Katherine and her father were partners in keeping chickens. Her father bought the chickens and the feed for them, while she took care of them. She was to have .4 of the eggs. Of the 300 eggs they gathered one month, how many were hers?

Multiplying Decimals 

235 300 .4 120.0 .4 of 300 eggs means .4 X 300 eggs.

Point off (count off) one decimal place in the product. 

She had 120 eggs. 

Why may you cross off the decimal 0 at the end of 120.0 without changing the number?

To multiply decimals, first multiply as with whole numbers. Then, beginning at the right, point off in the product as many decimal places as there are in both factors together. Finally, cross off all decimal zeros at the end of the product. 

Cautions: Never cross off the zeros at the end of a product until you have placed the decimal point. 

Cross off decimal zeros only; not zeros to the left of the decimal point.

Multiplying and Dividing Decimals 

241 

A Short Way of Multiplying and Dividing Decimals by 10 

or 100 (1) To multiply a whole number by 10, you write 0 on the end of the number (10 X 46 460); but you cannot multiply a decimal number by 10 in that way. 

To multiply a decimal number by 10, move the decimal point one place to the right. 

10 X 4.6 46. There is no use in leaving a decimal point at the end of a number. 10 X 4.6 46 (2) To multiply a decimal number by 100, move the decimal point two places to the right. 100 X 3.62 = 362

Often you have to annex a decimal 0 in order to be able to move the decimal point the required number of places. 100 X 3.6 = 360 (3) To divide a number by 10, move the decimal point one place to the left. 7.5 ÷ 10 = .75 

To move the decimal point in 9, think: 9 and 9. are the same. 

9 ÷ 10 = .9 (4) To divide a number by 100, move the decimal point two places to the left. 

937 100 = 9.37 

95 100 = .95 9 ÷ 100 = .09

CHAPTER VIII TESTING YOUR SKILL Telling How to Solve Problems 

Tell in a few words how to solve each of the following problems: 

1. If you know what one pound of beefsteak costs, how will you find the cost of 34 pounds? 

2. If you know how much Ned paid for each newspaper and how much he sold each one for, how can you find how much he made on 60 papers? 

3. If you know how much Alfred spent for a suit, how much for a pair of shoes, and how much for a cap, and that he gave the storekeeper $20 to pay for these items, how would you find how much change he should receive? 

4. If you know how many feet Tom can jump and how many feet farther than this Dan can jump, how will you find how far Dan can jump? 

5. If you know how many feet Polly can throw the basket ball and how many feet Nora can throw it, how can you find how much farther one girl can throw it than the other girl? 

6. If you know the number of pounds of peanuts in a certain sack, how can you find how many -pound bags of peanuts can be filled from the sack? 248

Solving Problems 249 

Reading Problems Better in Order to Solve Them Better 

Always read a problem through carefully before you start to work it. Read it several times, if necessary, to be sure you know just what it means. Then, if you cannot see how to work it, ask yourself these questions: 

1. What facts are stated in the problem ? 

2. What am I asked to find? 

3. How am I going to use the facts to find the answer? Is the situation one that requires adding, subtracting, multiplying, or dividing? 

What process or processes should I use to solve an easier problem of the same kind? 

Whenever it is possible, estimate your answer before you work the problem. After you have worked it, be sure that the answer is reasonable. 

Practice on these problems. The practice will help to increase your skill in reading problems to understand the situation. It will help you also in determining the processes to use and in making estimates. 

1. At 36¢ a pound, find the cost of 31 lb. of beefsteak. 

2. Tom was practicing the running broad jump. The first time he tried, he measured and found he had jumped 

93 ft. On the second trial, he jumped 21 ft. farther than the first time. How long a jump was that? 

3. Polly can can throw it 40 is Nora's throw? throw the basket ball 341 ft., and Nora ft. How much longer than Polly's throw 

4. When an airplane averages 107.5 miles an hour, how many miles does it go in 8 hr.?

5. The fifth-grade pupils were cutting strips of red paper to decorate boxes for a Valentine party. They had a roll of red paper 5 yards long. How many 6-inch strips could they cut from the paper? 

6. Jimmy rode his bicycle 13.7 miles on Friday and 18.2 miles on Saturday. He rode miles farther on Saturday than on Friday. 

7. Where Jean lived, .09 in. of rain fell in July, and 8 in. of rain fell in March. How many more inches of rain fell in March than in July? 

8. The speedometer on our automobile read 8946.6 miles before a 2-hour ride and 9000 miles at the end of the ride. We averaged miles per hour. 

9. After Herbert had spent $4.50 for a pair of shoes, $18 for a suit, $1.25 for a cap, and $.85 for gloves, he had $6.10 left. How much did he have at first?

19 of the eggs hatched. How many eggs hatched?

Cost of a Month's Schooling 

John wanted to find out how much it cost the city to keep him in school. The superintendent gave him the following 

1. For the 32 children in his grade, the city spent $2.88 a month for paper. What was John's share? 

2. Each pupil used 2 pencils a month. These pencils cost 24¢ a dozen. What did John's pencils cost a month? 

3. During the school year, the following supplies were used by the 32 pupils: chalk costing $3.90, ink costing $6, pens costing $7.22, erasers costing $0.75, drawing supplies costing $20.65, other supplies costing $23.88. 

What was

John's share of this expense for the year? 

4. Books costing $83.20 were purchased for the fifth grade during the year. What was the average cost of books for John for the year? 

5. The fifth-grade share of other expenses of running the school (such as salaries of teachers, principal, janitor; also heat, light, and cleaning) was $2080 per year of 10 months. What was the fifth-grade share per month? 

What part of this was John's share?

Solving Problems 

255 6. There were 307 children in the whole school. Textbooks for the school cost $859.60 per year of 10 months. 

What was the average cost of textbooks per pupil per year? 

Per month? 

7. School supplies for the year cost $706.10. the cost of supplies per pupil per month? 

What was 8. In John's school, the total cost of running the school for a year was $29,723.74. The superintendent told John that the average annual cost per pupil in their city was $103.12. Was the cost in John's school more or less than the average? How much? 

9. Find for your school the cost of textbooks, supplies, and other items, and report to the class the average costs per pupil.

Thrift Problems 

1. Dick has a savings account in a bank. He puts $1.50 into the bank every month. How much does he put in every year? How much in 5 years? 

2. When each dollar in a savings account earns 4¢ a year interest, how much interest will $18 earn in a year? 

3. Marian deposits 5¢ every school day in the school bank. Counting 5 school days in the week and 36 weeks in the school year, find how much Marian will deposit in a year. 

4. Mary's father earns $400 a month. He spends $75 for food, $60 for rent, $70 for clothing, and $56 for other expenses each month, and saves the rest of his salary. How much does he save each month?

5. Henry's father earns $100 a month. He plans to spend $20 a month for rent, $35 for food, $15 for clothing, $20 for other expenses, and to save the rest. How many dollars does he plan to save? What part of the $100 does he plan to save? 

6. Mr. Bowen earns $3000 a year. He plans to save of this. How much does he plan to save each month on the average? 

7. Mr. Burton earns $120 a month. He plans to spend of this for rent, of it for food, for clothing, for other expenses, and to save the rest. Find how many dollars he plans to spend each month for rent, for food, for clothing, and for other expenses, and also how many dollars he plans to save. 

8. Mrs. Wood bought an 8-yard remnant of cloth for $1.25. The regular price for this cloth was 18¢ per yard.

How much money did Mrs. Wood save when she bought the remnant? 

9. A heated apartment rents for $40 a month. A small house rents for $30 a month, but during a year it takes 8 tons of coal at $12.50 a ton to heat the house. Which is cheaper per year, the apartment or the house? How much cheaper is it? 

10. A family having an income of $5000 per year planned to spend of their income for rent, for food, for clothing, 20 for other things, and to save the rest. What part of this income did they plan to save? How much money did they plan to save?

Tests in Telling How to Work Examples 

The next tests will show you how much you know about how to work examples in whole numbers, fractions, and decimals.

In each test the answers with which to finish the statements are given below the statements. The answers are not in the same order as the statements. For example, in Test I, answer (e) in the second column completes the first statement: "1. To check an addition example, add in the other direction." 

Read each statement and think out the right answer. 

When you feel sure you know, choose the answer which fits from the set below and write the complete sentence. You may make the answer more complete, if you wish.

Matching Tests 

259 

TEST I 

1. To check an addition example, 2. To check a subtraction example, 3. To check a multiplication example, 4. To check a division example, 5. To add fractions, (a) Make them similar and add the numerators. (b) Multiply the quotient and the divisor, and add the remainder, if any. (c) Add the remainder and the subtrahend. (d) Interchange the multiplicand and multiplier, and multiply again. (e) Add in the other direction.

TEST II 

1. To subtract fractions, 2. To multiply fractions, 3. To divide by a fraction, 4. To change a fraction to higher terms, 5. To reduce a fraction to lower terms, 6. In multiplying mixed numbers, first (a) Multiply the numerators together and the denominators together. (b) Make them similar and subtract the numerators. (c) Change them to improper fractions. (d) Divide both terms by the same number. (e) Invert the divisor and multiply. (f) Multiply both terms by the same number.

TEST III 

1. To divide a decimal by 10, 2. To add decimals, first 

3. To multiply a decimal by 100, 4. To subtract decimals, first 

5. To divide a decimal by a whole number, first. 

6. To multiply a decimal by 10, 7. To divide a number by 100, 8. In multiplying decimals, (a) Place the decimal point in the quotient above the decimal point in the dividend. (b) Arrange with tenths under tenths, hundredths under hundredths, decimal point under decimal point, with the larger number on top. (c) Move the decimal point one place to the right. (d) Move the decimal point two places to the right. (e) Write the numbers with the decimal points in the same column. (f) Point off as many decimal places in the product as there are decimal places in the multiplicand and multiplier. (g) Move the decimal point one place to the left. (h) Move the decimal point two places to the left. 99
